--- guideline subpage (book/src/to-contribute/coding-guidelines/for-documentation/general-style.md) ---

# General Style

### Prefer semantic line breaks (`semantic-line-breaks`) {#semantic-line-breaks}

For prose in Markdown and doc comments,
insert line breaks at semantic boundaries
so each line carries one coherent idea.
At minimum, break at sentence boundaries.
For longer sentences, also consider breaking at clause boundaries.

Semantic line breaks make diffs smaller,
reviews easier,
and merge conflicts less noisy.

As an exception,
RFC documents that are mostly read-only
can use regular paragraph wrapping.

See also:
[Semantic Line Breaks](https://sembr.org/).

### Make a crate's `README.md` its crate-level documentation (`readme-as-crate-doc`) {#readme-as-crate-doc}

A published crate's `README.md` (shown on crates.io)
and its crate-level Rust doc (shown on docs.rs)
usually carry the same content.
Keep a single source of truth:
write the `README.md`,
and include it as the crate-level doc rather than maintaining a separate copy.

```rust
#![doc = include_str!("../README.md")]
```

Write the `README.md` so it renders correctly under both a Markdown renderer and rustdoc.

See also:
[Issue #2947](https://github.com/asterinas/asterinas/issues/2947)
for the rationale, the caveats, and a template;
the [`ostd-pod`](https://github.com/asterinas/asterinas/tree/main/ostd/libs/ostd-pod) crate
for a crate that already adopts it.


--- guideline subpage (book/src/to-contribute/coding-guidelines/for-documentation/path-specific/README.md) ---

# Path-Specific

Guidelines that apply only to code under a specific repository path.

- [`kernel/`](kernel.md)


--- guideline subpage (book/src/to-contribute/coding-guidelines/for-documentation/path-specific/kernel.md) ---

# `kernel/`

Guidelines that apply only to code under `kernel/`.

### Keep the Linux Compatibility docs current (`linux-compat-docs`) {#linux-compat-docs}

When a change under `kernel/` adds or enhances a user-visible API — a system call or a kernel parameter —
update the [Linux Compatibility](../../../../kernel/linux-compatibility/) section in the same PR,
so the documented coverage keeps matching the code.

- For a **system call**:
  update the matching [Syscall Flag Coverage](../../../../kernel/linux-compatibility/syscall-flag-coverage/) page
  and its [SCML](../../../../kernel/linux-compatibility/syscall-flag-coverage/system-call-matching-language.md) (`.scml`) coverage file
  to reflect the newly supported flags, arguments, and behaviors.
- For a **kernel parameter**:
  update the [Kernel Parameters](../../../../kernel/linux-compatibility/kernel-parameters.md) page.

     8	pub fn sys_madvise(
     9	    start: Vaddr,
    10	    len: usize,
    11	    behavior: i32,
    12	    ctx: &Context,
    13	) -> Result<SyscallReturn> {
    14	    let behavior = MadviseBehavior::try_from(behavior)?;
    15	    debug!(
    16	        "start = 0x{:x}, len = 0x{:x}, behavior = {:?}",
    17	        start, len, behavior
    18	    );
    19	
    20	    if start % PAGE_SIZE != 0 {
    21	        return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EINVAL, "the start address should be page aligned");
    22	    }
    23	    if len > isize::MAX as usize {
    24	        return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EINVAL, "len align overflow");
    25	    }
    26	    if len == 0 {
    27	        return Ok(SyscallReturn::Return(0));
    28	    }
    29	
    30	    let len = len.align_up(PAGE_SIZE);
    31	    let end = start.checked_add(len).ok_or(Error::with_message(
    32	        Errno::EINVAL,
    33	        "integer overflow when (start + len)",
    34	    ))?;
    35	    match behavior {
    36	        MadviseBehavior::MADV_NORMAL
    37	        | MadviseBehavior::MADV_SEQUENTIAL
    38	        | MadviseBehavior::MADV_WILLNEED => {

    53	fn madv_free(start: Vaddr, end: Vaddr, ctx: &Context) -> Result<()> {
    54	    let user_space = ctx.user_space();
    55	    let root_vmar = user_space.root_vmar();
    56	    let advised_range = start..end;
    57	    let _ = root_vmar.remove_mapping(advised_range);
    58	
    59	    Ok(())
    60	}

    16	    let user_space = ctx.user_space();
    17	    let cap_user_header: cap_user_header_t =
    18	        user_space.read_val::<cap_user_header_t>(cap_user_header_addr)?;
    19	
    20	    if cap_user_header.version != LINUX_CAPABILITY_VERSION_3 {
    21	        return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EINVAL, "not supported (capability version is not 3)");
    22	    };
    23	
    24	    // Extract target pid and validate whether it represents the current process.
    25	    let header_pid = cap_user_header.pid;
    26	    // Capget only query current process's credential. Namely, it only allows header->pid == 0
    27	    // or header->pid == getpid(), which are equivalent.
    28	    // See https://linux.die.net/man/2/capget (Section. With VFS capability support) for details.
    29	    if header_pid != 0 && header_pid != ctx.process.pid() {
    30	        return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EINVAL, "invalid pid");
    31	    }
    32	
    33	    let credentials = ctx.posix_thread.credentials();
    34	    let inheritable_capset = credentials.inheritable_capset();
    35	    let permitted_capset = credentials.permitted_capset();
    36	    let effective_capset = credentials.effective_capset();
    37	
    38	    // Annoying legacy format with 64-bit capabilities exposed as two sets of 32-bit fields,
    39	    // so we need to split the capability values up.
    40	    let result = cap_user_data_t {
    41	        // Note we silently drop the upper capabilities here.
    42	        // This behavior is considered fail-safe behavior.
    43	        effective: effective_capset.as_u32(),
    44	        permitted: permitted_capset.as_u32(),
    45	        inheritable: inheritable_capset.as_u32(),
    46	    };
    47	
    48	    user_space.write_val(cap_user_data_addr, &result)?;
    49	    Ok(SyscallReturn::Return(0))
    50	}

===== kernel/src/syscall/open.rs =====
     1	// SPDX-License-Identifier: MPL-2.0
     2	
     3	use super::SyscallReturn;
     4	use crate::{
     5	    fs::{
     6	        file_table::{FdFlags, FileDesc},
     7	        fs_resolver::{FsPath, AT_FDCWD},
     8	        utils::{AccessMode, CreationFlags},
     9	    },
    10	    prelude::*,
    11	    syscall::constants::MAX_FILENAME_LEN,
    12	};
    13	
    14	pub fn sys_openat(
    15	    dirfd: FileDesc,
    16	    path_addr: Vaddr,
    17	    flags: u32,
    18	    mode: u16,
    19	    ctx: &Context,
    20	) -> Result<SyscallReturn> {
    21	    let path = ctx.user_space().read_cstring(path_addr, MAX_FILENAME_LEN)?;
    22	    debug!(
    23	        "dirfd = {}, path = {:?}, flags = {}, mode = {}",
    24	        dirfd, path, flags, mode
    25	    );
    26	
    27	    let file_handle = {
    28	        let path = path.to_string_lossy();
    29	        let fs_path = FsPath::new(dirfd, path.as_ref())?;
    30	        let fs_ref = ctx.thread_local.borrow_fs();
    31	        let mask_mode = mode & !fs_ref.umask().read().get();
    32	        let inode_handle = fs_ref
    33	            .resolver()
    34	            .read()
    35	            .open(&fs_path, flags, mask_mode)
    36	            .map_err(|err| match err.error() {
    37	                Errno::EINTR => Error::new(Errno::ERESTARTSYS),
    38	                _ => err,
    39	            })?;
    40	        Arc::new(inode_handle)
    41	    };
    42	
    43	    let fd = {
    44	        let file_table = ctx.thread_local.borrow_file_table();
    45	        let mut file_table_locked = file_table.unwrap().write();
    46	        let fd_flags =
    47	            if CreationFlags::from_bits_truncate(flags).contains(CreationFlags::O_CLOEXEC) {
    48	                FdFlags::CLOEXEC
    49	            } else {
    50	                FdFlags::empty()
    51	            };
    52	        file_table_locked.insert(file_handle, fd_flags)
    53	    };
    54	
    55	    Ok(SyscallReturn::Return(fd as _))
    56	}
    57	
    58	pub fn sys_open(path_addr: Vaddr, flags: u32, mode: u16, ctx: &Context) -> Result<SyscallReturn> {
    59	    self::sys_openat(AT_FDCWD, path_addr, flags, mode, ctx)
    60	}
    61	
    62	pub fn sys_creat(path_addr: Vaddr, mode: u16, ctx: &Context) -> Result<SyscallReturn> {
    63	    let flags =
    64	        AccessMode::O_WRONLY as u32 | CreationFlags::O_CREAT.bits() | CreationFlags::O_TRUNC.bits();
    65	    self::sys_openat(AT_FDCWD, path_addr, flags, mode, ctx)
    66	}

   146	### `madvise`
   147	
   148	Supported functionality in SCML:
   149	
   150	```c
   151	// Apply the default memory access pattern with no special optimizations
   152	madvise(addr, length, advice = MADV_NORMAL);
   153	
   154	// Indicate sequential access to enable aggressive read-ahead and immediate page release
   155	madvise(addr, length, advice = MADV_SEQUENTIAL);
   156

   159	```

     1	# File and Directory Operations
     2	
     3	<!--
     4	Put system calls such as
     5	open, openat, creat, close, read, write, readv, writev, pread64, 
     6	pwrite64, lseek, stat, fstat, lstat, statx, mkdir, rmdir, link, 
     7	unlink, rename, symlink, readlink, chmod, fchmod, chown, fchown, 
     8	utime, and utimensat
     9	under this category.
    10	-->
    11	
    12	## `open` and `openat`
    13	
    14	Supported functionality of `open` in SCML:
    15	
    16	```c
    17	access_mode =
    18	    O_RDONLY |
    19	    O_WRONLY |
    20	    O_RDWR;
    21	creation_flags =
    22	    O_CLOEXEC |
    23	    O_DIRECTORY |
    24	    O_EXCL |
    25	    O_NOCTTY |
    26	    O_NOFOLLOW |
    27	    O_TRUNC;
    28	status_flags =
    29	    O_APPEND |
    30	    O_ASYNC |
    31	    O_DIRECT |
    32	    O_LARGEFILE |
    33	    O_NOATIME |
    34	    O_NONBLOCK |
    35	    O_SYNC;
    36	
    37	// Open an existing file
    38	open(
    39	    path,
    40	    flags = <access_mode> | <creation_flags> | <status_flags>,
    41	);
    42	
    43	// Create a new file
    44	open(
    45	    path,
    46	    flags = O_CREAT | <access_mode> | <creation_flags> | <status_flags>,
    47	    mode
    48	);
    49	
    50	// Status flags that are meaningful with O_PATH
    51	opath_valid_flags = O_CLOEXEC | O_DIRECTORY | O_NOFOLLOW;
    52	// All other flags are ignored with O_PATH
    53	opath_ignored_flags = O_CREAT | <creation_flags> | <status_flags>;
    54	// Obtain a file descriptor to indicate a location in FS
    55	open(
    56	    path,
    57	    flags = O_PATH | <opath_valid_flags> | <opath_ignored_flags>
    58	);
    59	
    60	// Create an unnamed file 
    61	// open(path, flags = O_TMPFILE | <creation_flags> | <status_flags>) 
    62	```
    63	
    64	Silently-ignored flags:
    65	* `O_NOCTTY`
    66	* `O_DSYNC`
    67	* `O_SYNC`
    68	* `O_LARGEFILE`
    69	* `O_NOATIME`
    70	* `O_NOCTTY`
    71	
    72	Partially-supported flags:
    73	* `O_PATH`
    74	
    75	Unsupported flags:
    76	* `O_TMPFILE`
    77	
    78	Supported and unsupported functionality of `openat` are the same as `open`.
    79	The SCML rules are omitted for brevity.
    80	
    81	For more information,
    82	see [the man page](https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man2/openat.2.html).

    /// Clears all content of the root VMAR.
    fn clear_root_vmar(&self) -> Result<()> {
        let mut inner = self.inner.write();
        inner.vm_mappings.clear();

        // Keep `inner` locked to avoid race conditions.
        let preempt_guard = disable_preempt();
        let full_range = 0..MAX_USERSPACE_VADDR;
        let mut cursor = self
            .vm_space
            .cursor_mut(&preempt_guard, &full_range)
            .unwrap();
        cursor.unmap(full_range.len());
        cursor.flusher().sync_tlb_flush();

        Ok(())
    }

    pub fn remove_mapping(&self, range: Range<usize>) -> Result<()> {
        let mut inner = self.inner.write();
        let mut rss_delta = RssDelta::new(self);
        inner.alloc_free_region_exact_truncate(
            &self.vm_space,
            range.start,
            range.len(),
            &mut rss_delta,
        )?;
        Ok(())
    }

    /// Splits and unmaps the found mapping if the new size is smaller.
    /// Enlarges the last mapping if the new size is larger.
    fn resize_mapping(
        &self,
        map_addr: Vaddr,
        old_size: usize,
        new_size: usize,

    let user_space = ctx.user_space();
    let root_vmar = user_space.root_vmar();
    let len = len.align_up(PAGE_SIZE);
    let end = addr.checked_add(len).ok_or(Error::with_message(
        Errno::EINVAL,
        "integer overflow when (addr + len)",
    ))?;
    debug!("unmap range = 0x{:x} - 0x{:x}", addr, end);
    root_vmar.remove_mapping(addr..end)?;
    Ok(SyscallReturn::Return(0))
}

// SPDX-License-Identifier: MPL-2.0

use align_ext::AlignExt;

use super::SyscallReturn;
use crate::prelude::*;

pub fn sys_munmap(addr: Vaddr, len: usize, ctx: &Context) -> Result<SyscallReturn> {
    debug!("addr = 0x{:x}, len = {}", addr, len);

    if addr % PAGE_SIZE != 0 {
        return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EINVAL, "munmap addr must be page-aligned");
    }
    if len == 0 {
        return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EINVAL, "munmap len cannot be zero");
    }
    if len > isize::MAX as usize {
        return_errno_with_message!(Errno::ENOMEM, "munmap len align overflow");
    }

    let user_space = ctx.user_space();
    let root_vmar = user_space.root_vmar();
    let len = len.align_up(PAGE_SIZE);
    let end = addr.checked_add(len).ok_or(Error::with_message(
        Errno::EINVAL,
        "integer overflow when (addr + len)",
    ))?;
    debug!("unmap range = 0x{:x} - 0x{:x}", addr, end);
    root_vmar.remove_mapping(addr..end)?;
    Ok(SyscallReturn::Return(0))
}

// SPDX-License-Identifier: MPL-2.0

use core::sync::atomic::{AtomicU64, Ordering};

use atomic_integer_wrapper::define_atomic_version_of_integer_like_type;
use bitflags::bitflags;

bitflags! {
    /// Represents a set of Linux capabilities.
    pub struct CapSet: u64 {
        const CHOWN = 1 << 0;
        const DAC_OVERRIDE = 1 << 1;
        const DAC_READ_SEARCH = 1 << 2;
        const FOWNER = 1 << 3;
        const FSETID = 1 << 4;
        const KILL = 1 << 5;
        const SETGID = 1 << 6;
        const SETUID = 1 << 7;
        const SETPCAP = 1 << 8;
        const LINUX_IMMUTABLE = 1 << 9;
        const NET_BIND_SERVICE = 1 << 10;
        const NET_BROADCAST = 1 << 11;
        const NET_ADMIN = 1 << 12;
        const NET_RAW = 1 << 13;
        const IPC_LOCK = 1 << 14;
        const IPC_OWNER = 1 << 15;
        const SYS_MODULE = 1 << 16;
        const SYS_RAWIO = 1 << 17;
        const SYS_CHROOT = 1 << 18;
        const SYS_PTRACE = 1 << 19;
        const SYS_PACCT = 1 << 20;
        const SYS_ADMIN = 1 << 21;
        const SYS_BOOT = 1 << 22;
        const SYS_NICE = 1 << 23;
        const SYS_RESOURCE = 1 << 24;
        const SYS_TIME = 1 << 25;
        const SYS_TTY_CONFIG = 1 << 26;
        const MKNOD = 1 << 27;
        const LEASE = 1 << 28;
        const AUDIT_WRITE = 1 << 29;
        const AUDIT_CONTROL = 1 << 30;
        const SETFCAP = 1 << 31;
        const MAC_OVERRIDE = 1 << 32;
        const MAC_ADMIN = 1 << 33;
        const SYSLOG = 1 << 34;
        const WAKE_ALARM = 1 << 35;
        const BLOCK_SUSPEND = 1 << 36;
        const AUDIT_READ = 1 << 37;
        const PERFMON = 1 << 38;
        const BPF = 1 << 39;
        const CHECKPOINT_RESTORE = 1u64 << 40;
        // ... include other capabilities as needed
    }
}

impl CapSet {
    const MASK: u64 = (1 << (CapSet::most_significant_bit() + 1)) - 1;

    /// Converts the capability set to a `u32`. The higher bits are truncated.
    pub fn as_u32(&self) -> u32 {
        self.bits() as u32
    }

    /// Creates a new `CapSet` with a full capability set, typically for a root user.
    pub const fn new_root() -> Self {
        CapSet::all()
    }

    /// The most significant bit in a 64-bit `CapSet` that may be set to represent a Linux capability.
    pub const fn most_significant_bit() -> u8 {
        // CHECKPOINT_RESTORE is the Linux capability with the largest numerical value
        40
    }
}

impl TryFrom<u64> for CapSet {
    type Error = &'static str;

    fn try_from(value: u64) -> Result<Self, Self::Error> {
        if value & !CapSet::MASK != 0 {
            Err("Invalid CapSet.")
        } else {
            Ok(CapSet { bits: value })
        }
    }
}

impl From<CapSet> for u64 {
    fn from(value: CapSet) -> Self {
        value.bits()

#!/usr/bin/env bash

# SPDX-License-Identifier: MPL-2.0

#
# assemble_review.sh — deterministic merge of persona comment fragments into the review file body.
# No LLM judgement here:
# group by persona in a fixed order,
# sort by file then line within each group,
# drop exact duplicates *within a persona*,
# write the frontmatter,
# and leave a <!-- SUMMARY --> placeholder for the orchestrator's LLM summary step.
#
# Usage: assemble_review.sh [--overwrite] <meta-file> <frag-dir> <output-file>
#   meta-file: lines `mode=`, `base=` (diff) or `files=` (files), `head=`,
#              `branch=`, `date=`, `title=`
#   frag-dir:  one <persona>.json per persona, each a JSON array of comments:
#       {"file","line","grounding","severity","problem","fix","diff"}
#   --overwrite: replace <output-file> if it already exists (otherwise fail).
set -euo pipefail

overwrite=0
pos=()
for a in "$@"; do
    case "$a" in
        --overwrite) overwrite=1 ;;
        --*) echo "assemble_review.sh: unknown flag: $a" >&2; exit 2 ;;
        *) pos+=("$a") ;;
    esac
done
if [[ ${#pos[@]} -ne 3 ]]; then
    echo "usage: assemble_review.sh [--overwrite] <meta-file> <frag-dir> <output-file>" >&2
    exit 2
fi
meta="${pos[0]}"; fragdir="${pos[1]}"; out="${pos[2]}"

# Refuse to clobber an existing review unless --overwrite (design §3).
if [[ -e "$out" && $overwrite -eq 0 ]]; then
    echo "assemble_review.sh: refusing to overwrite existing $out (pass --overwrite)" >&2
    exit 1
fi

python3 - "$meta" "$fragdir" "$out" <<'PY'
import json, os, re, sys
meta_path, fragdir, out = sys.argv[1], sys.argv[2], sys.argv[3]

# A guideline short-name is a lowercase kebab identifier (e.g. `lock-ordering`) and
# renders as code — a citable rule. A bug's grounding is a short plain-language
# description (e.g. "Off by one") and renders as prose, so it never reads as a rule name.
KEBAB = re.compile(r"[a-z0-9]+(-[a-z0-9]+)*")
def grounding_tag(g):
    return "`%s`" % g if KEBAB.fullmatch(g) else g

meta = {}
for line in open(meta_path):
    line = line.strip()
    if "=" in line:
        k, v = line.split("=", 1)
        meta[k] = v

# Fixed persona order -> review section title.
# "Correctness" is the Development persona's section (a bare "## Development" reads oddly; see the design doc).
ORDER = [("maintainability", "Maintainability"),
         ("development",      "Correctness"),
         ("security",         "Security"),
         ("hardware",         "Hardware"),
         ("documentation",    "Documentation")]

def load(persona):
    p = os.path.join(fragdir, persona + ".json")
    if not os.path.exists(p):
        return []          # persona not activated / did not run: legitimately absent
    # An EXISTING fragment that is unparseable or not a JSON array means a pass
    # produced broken output. Failing closed (rather than dropping it) is essential:
    # this skill is recall-first, so a silently-discarded fragment is a silent
    # recall hole the benchmark/orchestrator would never notice.
    try:
        data = json.load(open(p))
    except Exception as e:
        sys.stderr.write(f"assemble: FATAL: unparseable fragment {p}: {e}\n")
        sys.exit(2)
    if not isinstance(data, list):
        sys.stderr.write(f"assemble: FATAL: fragment {p} is not a JSON array of comments\n")
        sys.exit(2)
    return data

L = ["---",
     f"date: {meta.get('date','')}",
     f"mode: {meta.get('mode','diff')}"]
if meta.get("base"):
    L.append(f"base: {meta['base']}")
if meta.get("files"):
    L.append(f"files: {meta['files']}")
L += [f"head: {meta.get('head','')}",
      f"branch: {meta.get('branch','')}"]
if meta.get("title"):
    # json.dumps yields a valid YAML double-quoted scalar (escapes embedded quotes).
    L.append("title: " + json.dumps(meta["title"]))
L += ["---", "", "# Summary", "", "<!-- SUMMARY -->", ""]

counts = {}
for persona, title in ORDER:
    # Dedup is per-persona: identical comments from two different personas are both kept,
    # in their own sections (design §4).
    seen = set()
    uniq = []
    for c in load(persona):
        # "Exact" duplicate: identical in EVERY field (a normalized full-object key),
        # so two comments that differ in grounding, severity, or diff are both kept.
        # A partial key would collapse distinct findings — a recall hole.
        key = json.dumps(c, sort_keys=True)
        if key in seen:
            continue
        seen.add(key)
        uniq.append(c)
    counts[persona] = len(uniq)
    if not uniq:
        continue
    # Sort by file, then line, then a stable text key
    # so co-located comments have a deterministic order regardless of pass output order.
    uniq.sort(key=lambda c: (str(c.get("file", "")),
                             int(c.get("line", 0) or 0),
                             str(c.get("grounding", "")),
                             str(c.get("problem", ""))))
    L += [f"## {title}", ""]
    for c in uniq:
        loc = "`%s`" % c.get("file", "?")
        if c.get("line"):
            loc += " line %s" % c["line"]
        L += [f"### {loc}", ""]
        diff = c.get("diff")
        if diff:
            L.append("> ```diff")
            L += ["> " + dl for dl in str(diff).splitlines()]
            L += ["> ```", ""]
        L.append("%s (%s): %s" % (grounding_tag(c.get("grounding", "issue")),
                                  c.get("severity", "major"),
                                  (c.get("problem", "") or "").strip()))
        L += ["", "**Fix.** %s" % (c.get("fix", "") or "").strip(), ""]

open(out, "w").write("\n".join(L).rstrip() + "\n")
sys.stderr.write("assemble: " + ", ".join(f"{p}={counts[p]}" for p, _ in ORDER) + "\n")
PY

# Pass contract

You are a reviewer applying the persona guideline(s) included below to the change or files under review
(the **REVIEW INPUT** at the very end of this prompt).
Find as many real defects as possible within the included persona(s)' remit
— runtime correctness for Development,
security/soundness for Security,
ABI/hardware for Hardware,
doc style/currency for Documentation,
and structure/process for Maintainability
— without inventing issues; a false alarm is a real cost.

For each persona block below,
work that persona's concerns in the order its file gives.
For each candidate rule,
read its one-line gist first
and drill into the full rule (its linked subsections) only on a suspected violation.
Stay within the remit of the persona(s) you are given.

Each persona searches only for defects whose failure belongs to that persona.
Do not run a general bug sweep from every persona.
When another persona is the clear natural owner,
do not duplicate that investigation here.
For example,
Maintainability should inspect design shape, readability, naming, layout,
and commit hygiene;
it should not trace runtime permission semantics, Linux/POSIX behavior,
wrong predicates, or data-flow edge cases unless they are evidence of a
maintainability rule violation.

Within each included persona's owned failure modes,
reason about the code even when no explicit guideline names the issue.
Examples include off-by-one and reachable panic for Development,
input-validation or permission-boundary flaws for Security,
ABI/alignment hazards for Hardware,
navigation or currency defects for Documentation,
and structural or process defects for Maintainability.
Ground each non-guideline finding in a short plain-language description of the defect
("Off by one", "Use after free", "Reachable panic", …)
— not the bare word `bug`, and not a coined hyphenated short-name,
which would read as a guideline.
Never stay silent about a real defect that belongs to the included persona
because "no guideline covers it".

Be **adversarial**:
before dismissing a suspected in-scope defect as safe,
state the concrete input or interleaving that would trigger it.
Report an in-scope defect unless you can show that case cannot happen.
"It looks fine" is not a verdict.

The REVIEW INPUT is the unit of review;
you MAY read surrounding code in the working tree for extra context.

## Output

Output **only** a JSON array of comment objects (no prose around it):

```json
[{"file":"path/relative/to/repo.rs","line":42,"persona":"development","grounding":"lock-ordering","severity":"major",
  "problem":"`foo()` takes `b.lock()` while already holding `a.lock()`, the reverse of the `a`-before-`b` order elsewhere — a deadlock",
  "fix":"take `a.lock()` before `b.lock()` here too, matching the rest of the module",
  "diff":"the few relevant lines (a diff hunk, or source lines in files mode)"}]
```

- `persona` — which persona section the comment belongs to (`maintainability`, `development`, `security`, `hardware`, `documentation`);
  used to file the comment under the right section.
  In a single-persona (fan-out) pass it is always that persona.
- `grounding` — what the comment rests on, in one of two forms kept visually distinct:
  when you **cite a guideline**, its short-name
  — a lowercase kebab identifier (e.g. `lock-ordering`), rendered as code;
  when you report a **bug no guideline covers**, a short plain-language description of the defect
  (e.g. "Off by one", "Use after free", "Incorrect cleanup"), rendered as prose.
  Do not coin a hyphenated pseudo-short-name for a bug
  — that reads as a guideline
  — and never use the bare word `bug`,
  which says nothing the reader cannot already see.
- `severity` — **required**,
  one of `critical` (must fix) / `major` (should fix) / `minor` (worth fixing) / `nit` (optional or stylistic).
- `problem` and `fix` are **both required**
  — every comment proposes a remedy.
  They are posted as GitHub-flavored Markdown,
  so wrap every code identifier, path, type, function or variable name, and literal value in backticks
  (`self.len`, `Ordering::Acquire`, `kernel/src/foo.rs`),
  and put any multi-line snippet in `fix` in a fenced ```` ``` ```` block.
  (The `grounding` of a bug stays plain prose, as described above
  — only `problem` and `fix` take inline code.)
- Every field above is required on every comment,
  with a single exception for `line`, described next.
- `line` is the line the comment anchors to
  — the post-change line in the commit's diff (`diff` mode),
  or the file's line number (`files` mode).
  It is **required for a finding about code**.
- For a finding about a **commit message** (`diff` mode shows each commit's message),
  set `file` to the commit locus (e.g. `commit abc1234 message`),
  **omit `line`** (its one exception),
  and ground it in a commit-hygiene rule (`imperative-subject`, `atomic-commits`, …).
- Report only issues within the included persona(s)' remit.
  If you find nothing, output `[]`.

===== PERSONA: documentation =====

# Documentation persona

**Review section:** Documentation
**Remit:** Are user-facing docs and compatibility artifacts correct, current, and well-written?

**Your guideline page (read only this, drill in on suspicion):**

— subsections: `general-style.md`, `path-specific/kernel.md`.

**Concerns, in order:**

1. **General style** — `semantic-line-breaks` (break prose at sentence/clause boundaries),
   `readme-as-crate-doc` (a published crate's `README.md` is its crate-level doc).

   — `linux-compat-docs`: when a change under `kernel/` alters a user-visible API (a syscall or a kernel parameter),
   the Linux Compatibility docs (Syscall Flag Coverage + `.scml`, or Kernel Parameters) must be updated in the same change.
   A code change that should have a matching doc update but does not is a defect you own.

You activate when the change touches docs, coverage files, or a user-facing API surface.
You own doc correctness and currency, not code behaviour.

Rely on the inlined guideline material below as the authoritative guideline context for this pass. Do not re-read `book/src/to-contribute/coding-guidelines/` from the worktree under review for guideline content.

# For Documentation

*Are user-facing docs and compatibility artifacts correct, current, and well-written?*

This is the index of the **documentation** guidelines.
Each subsection is its own page,
and each entry below links a stable `short-name` to its guideline,
with a one-line gist so a reader (or a review tool) can grasp the guideline before opening it.

## Index

**[General Style](general-style.md)**
- [`semantic-line-breaks`](general-style.md#semantic-line-breaks): Break prose at sentence/clause boundaries, one idea per line.
- [`readme-as-crate-doc`](general-style.md#readme-as-crate-doc): For a published crate, make `README.md` the crate-level doc (`#![doc = include_str!("../README.md")]`) so crates.io and docs.rs stay in sync.

**[Path-Specific](path-specific/)**
- [`kernel/`](path-specific/kernel.md)

--- guideline subpage (book/src/to-contribute/coding-guidelines/for-documentation/general-style.md) ---

# General Style

### Prefer semantic line breaks (`semantic-line-breaks`) {#semantic-line-breaks}

For prose in Markdown and doc comments,
insert line breaks at semantic boundaries
so each line carries one coherent idea.
At minimum, break at sentence boundaries.
For longer sentences, also consider breaking at clause boundaries.

Semantic line breaks make diffs smaller,
reviews easier,
and merge conflicts less noisy.

As an exception,
RFC documents that are mostly read-only
can use regular paragraph wrapping.

See also:
[Semantic Line Breaks](https://sembr.org/).

### Make a crate's `README.md` its crate-level documentation (`readme-as-crate-doc`) {#readme-as-crate-doc}

A published crate's `README.md` (shown on crates.io)
and its crate-level Rust doc (shown on docs.rs)
usually carry the same content.
Keep a single source of truth:
write the `README.md`,
and include it as the crate-level doc rather than maintaining a separate copy.

```rust
#![doc = include_str!("../README.md")]
```

Write the `README.md` so it renders correctly under both a Markdown renderer and rustdoc.

See also:
[Issue #2947](https://github.com/asterinas/asterinas/issues/2947)
for the rationale, the caveats, and a template;
the [`ostd-pod`](https://github.com/asterinas/asterinas/tree/main/ostd/libs/ostd-pod) crate
for a crate that already adopts it.


--- guideline subpage (book/src/to-contribute/coding-guidelines/for-documentation/path-specific/README.md) ---

# Path-Specific

Guidelines that apply only to code under a specific repository path.

- [`kernel/`](kernel.md)


--- guideline subpage (book/src/to-contribute/coding-guidelines/for-documentation/path-specific/kernel.md) ---

# `kernel/`

Guidelines that apply only to code under `kernel/`.

### Keep the Linux Compatibility docs current (`linux-compat-docs`) {#linux-compat-docs}

When a change under `kernel/` adds or enhances a user-visible API — a system call or a kernel parameter —
update the [Linux Compatibility](../../../../kernel/linux-compatibility/) section in the same PR,
so the documented coverage keeps matching the code.

- For a **system call**:
  update the matching [Syscall Flag Coverage](../../../../kernel/linux-compatibility/syscall-flag-coverage/) page
  and its [SCML](../../../../kernel/linux-compatibility/syscall-flag-coverage/system-call-matching-language.md) (`.scml`) coverage file
  to reflect the newly supported flags, arguments, and behaviors.
- For a **kernel parameter**:
  update the [Kernel Parameters](../../../../kernel/linux-compatibility/kernel-parameters.md) page.

===== REVIEW INPUT =====

===== kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs =====
     1	// SPDX-License-Identifier: MPL-2.0
     2	
     3	use align_ext::AlignExt;
     4	
     5	use super::SyscallReturn;
     6	use crate::prelude::*;
     7	
     8	pub fn sys_madvise(
     9	    start: Vaddr,
    10	    len: usize,
    11	    behavior: i32,
    12	    ctx: &Context,
    13	) -> Result<SyscallReturn> {
    14	    let behavior = MadviseBehavior::try_from(behavior)?;
    15	    debug!(
    16	        "start = 0x{:x}, len = 0x{:x}, behavior = {:?}",
    17	        start, len, behavior
    18	    );
    19	
    20	    if start % PAGE_SIZE != 0 {
    21	        return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EINVAL, "the start address should be page aligned");
    22	    }
    23	    if len > isize::MAX as usize {
    24	        return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EINVAL, "len align overflow");
    25	    }
    26	    if len == 0 {
    27	        return Ok(SyscallReturn::Return(0));
    28	    }
    29	
    30	    let len = len.align_up(PAGE_SIZE);
    31	    let end = start.checked_add(len).ok_or(Error::with_message(
    32	        Errno::EINVAL,
    33	        "integer overflow when (start + len)",
    34	    ))?;
    35	    match behavior {
    36	        MadviseBehavior::MADV_NORMAL
    37	        | MadviseBehavior::MADV_SEQUENTIAL
    38	        | MadviseBehavior::MADV_WILLNEED => {
    39	            // perform a read at first
    40	            let mut buffer = vec![0u8; len];
    41	            ctx.user_space()
    42	                .read_bytes(start, &mut VmWriter::from(buffer.as_mut_slice()))?;
    43	        }
    44	        MadviseBehavior::MADV_DONTNEED => {
    45	            warn!("MADV_DONTNEED isn't implemented, do nothing for now.");
    46	        }
    47	        MadviseBehavior::MADV_FREE => madv_free(start, end, ctx)?,
    48	        _ => todo!(),
    49	    }
    50	    Ok(SyscallReturn::Return(0))
    51	}
    52	
    53	fn madv_free(start: Vaddr, end: Vaddr, ctx: &Context) -> Result<()> {
    54	    let user_space = ctx.user_space();
    55	    let root_vmar = user_space.root_vmar();
    56	    let advised_range = start..end;
    57	    let _ = root_vmar.remove_mapping(advised_range);
    58	
    59	    Ok(())
    60	}
    61	
    62	#[repr(i32)]
    63	#[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, TryFromInt)]
    64	#[expect(non_camel_case_types)]
    65	/// This definition is the same from linux
    66	pub enum MadviseBehavior {
    67	    MADV_NORMAL = 0,     /* no further special treatment */
    68	    MADV_RANDOM = 1,     /* expect random page references */
    69	    MADV_SEQUENTIAL = 2, /* expect sequential page references */
    70	    MADV_WILLNEED = 3,   /* will need these pages */
    71	    MADV_DONTNEED = 4,   /* don't need these pages */
    72	
    73	    /* common parameters: try to keep these consistent across architectures */
    74	    MADV_FREE = 8,           /* free pages only if memory pressure */
    75	    MADV_REMOVE = 9,         /* remove these pages & resources */
    76	    MADV_DONTFORK = 10,      /* don't inherit across fork */
    77	    MADV_DOFORK = 11,        /* do inherit across fork */
    78	    MADV_HWPOISON = 100,     /* poison a page for testing */
    79	    MADV_SOFT_OFFLINE = 101, /* soft offline page for testing */
    80	
    81	    MADV_MERGEABLE = 12,   /* KSM may merge identical pages */
    82	    MADV_UNMERGEABLE = 13, /* KSM may not merge identical pages */
    83	
    84	    MADV_HUGEPAGE = 14,   /* Worth backing with hugepages */
    85	    MADV_NOHUGEPAGE = 15, /* Not worth backing with hugepages */
    86	
    87	    MADV_DONTDUMP = 16, /* Explicitly exclude from the core dump,
    88	                        overrides the coredump filter bits */
    89	    MADV_DODUMP = 17, /* Clear the MADV_DONTDUMP flag */
    90	
    91	    MADV_WIPEONFORK = 18, /* Zero memory on fork, child only */
    92	    MADV_KEEPONFORK = 19, /* Undo MADV_WIPEONFORK */
    93	
    94	    MADV_COLD = 20,    /* deactivate these pages */
    95	    MADV_PAGEOUT = 21, /* reclaim these pages */
    96	
    97	    MADV_POPULATE_READ = 22,  /* populate (prefault) page tables readable */
    98	    MADV_POPULATE_WRITE = 23, /* populate (prefault) page tables writable */
    99	
   100	    MADV_DONTNEED_LOCKED = 24, /* like DONTNEED, but drop locked pages too */
   101	}

===== kernel/src/syscall/capget.rs =====
     1	// SPDX-License-Identifier: MPL-2.0
     2	
     3	use super::SyscallReturn;
     4	use crate::{
     5	    prelude::*,
     6	    process::credentials::c_types::{
     7	        cap_user_data_t, cap_user_header_t, LINUX_CAPABILITY_VERSION_3,
     8	    },
     9	};
    10	
    11	pub fn sys_capget(
    12	    cap_user_header_addr: Vaddr,
    13	    cap_user_data_addr: Vaddr,
    14	    ctx: &Context,
    15	) -> Result<SyscallReturn> {
    16	    let user_space = ctx.user_space();
    17	    let cap_user_header: cap_user_header_t =
    18	        user_space.read_val::<cap_user_header_t>(cap_user_header_addr)?;
    19	
    20	    if cap_user_header.version != LINUX_CAPABILITY_VERSION_3 {
    21	        return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EINVAL, "not supported (capability version is not 3)");
    22	    };
    23	
    24	    // Extract target pid and validate whether it represents the current process.
    25	    let header_pid = cap_user_header.pid;
    26	    // Capget only query current process's credential. Namely, it only allows header->pid == 0
    27	    // or header->pid == getpid(), which are equivalent.
    28	    // See https://linux.die.net/man/2/capget (Section. With VFS capability support) for details.
    29	    if header_pid != 0 && header_pid != ctx.process.pid() {
    30	        return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EINVAL, "invalid pid");
    31	    }
    32	
    33	    let credentials = ctx.posix_thread.credentials();
    34	    let inheritable_capset = credentials.inheritable_capset();
    35	    let permitted_capset = credentials.permitted_capset();
    36	    let effective_capset = credentials.effective_capset();
    37	
    38	    // Annoying legacy format with 64-bit capabilities exposed as two sets of 32-bit fields,
    39	    // so we need to split the capability values up.
    40	    let result = cap_user_data_t {
    41	        // Note we silently drop the upper capabilities here.
    42	        // This behavior is considered fail-safe behavior.
    43	        effective: effective_capset.as_u32(),
    44	        permitted: permitted_capset.as_u32(),
    45	        inheritable: inheritable_capset.as_u32(),
    46	    };
    47	
    48	    user_space.write_val(cap_user_data_addr, &result)?;
    49	    Ok(SyscallReturn::Return(0))
    50	}

===== kernel/src/syscall/open.rs =====
     1	// SPDX-License-Identifier: MPL-2.0
     2	
     3	use super::SyscallReturn;
     4	use crate::{
     5	    fs::{
     6	        file_table::{FdFlags, FileDesc},
     7	        fs_resolver::{FsPath, AT_FDCWD},
     8	        utils::{AccessMode, CreationFlags},
     9	    },
    10	    prelude::*,
    11	    syscall::constants::MAX_FILENAME_LEN,
    12	};
    13	
    14	pub fn sys_openat(
    15	    dirfd: FileDesc,
    16	    path_addr: Vaddr,
    17	    flags: u32,
    18	    mode: u16,
    19	    ctx: &Context,
    20	) -> Result<SyscallReturn> {
    21	    let path = ctx.user_space().read_cstring(path_addr, MAX_FILENAME_LEN)?;
    22	    debug!(
    23	        "dirfd = {}, path = {:?}, flags = {}, mode = {}",
    24	        dirfd, path, flags, mode
    25	    );
    26	
    27	    let file_handle = {
    28	        let path = path.to_string_lossy();
    29	        let fs_path = FsPath::new(dirfd, path.as_ref())?;
    30	        let fs_ref = ctx.thread_local.borrow_fs();
    31	        let mask_mode = mode & !fs_ref.umask().read().get();
    32	        let inode_handle = fs_ref
    33	            .resolver()
    34	            .read()
    35	            .open(&fs_path, flags, mask_mode)
    36	            .map_err(|err| match err.error() {
    37	                Errno::EINTR => Error::new(Errno::ERESTARTSYS),
    38	                _ => err,
    39	            })?;
    40	        Arc::new(inode_handle)
    41	    };
    42	
    43	    let fd = {
    44	        let file_table = ctx.thread_local.borrow_file_table();
    45	        let mut file_table_locked = file_table.unwrap().write();
    46	        let fd_flags =
    47	            if CreationFlags::from_bits_truncate(flags).contains(CreationFlags::O_CLOEXEC) {
    48	                FdFlags::CLOEXEC
    49	            } else {
    50	                FdFlags::empty()
    51	            };
    52	        file_table_locked.insert(file_handle, fd_flags)
    53	    };
    54	
    55	    Ok(SyscallReturn::Return(fd as _))
    56	}
    57	
    58	pub fn sys_open(path_addr: Vaddr, flags: u32, mode: u16, ctx: &Context) -> Result<SyscallReturn> {
    59	    self::sys_openat(AT_FDCWD, path_addr, flags, mode, ctx)
    60	}
    61	
    62	pub fn sys_creat(path_addr: Vaddr, mode: u16, ctx: &Context) -> Result<SyscallReturn> {
    63	    let flags =
    64	        AccessMode::O_WRONLY as u32 | CreationFlags::O_CREAT.bits() | CreationFlags::O_TRUNC.bits();
    65	    self::sys_openat(AT_FDCWD, path_addr, flags, mode, ctx)
    66	}

    /// This method will panic if the current process has cleared its `Vmar`.
    pub fn root_vmar(&self) -> &Vmar<Full> {
        self.0.as_ref().unwrap()
    }

    /// Creates a reader to read data from the user space of the current task.
    ///
    /// Returns `Err` if the `vaddr` and `len` do not represent a user space memory range.
    pub fn reader(&self, vaddr: Vaddr, len: usize) -> Result<VmReader<'_, Fallible>> {
        Ok(self.root_vmar().vm_space().reader(vaddr, len)?)
    }

    /// Creates a writer to write data into the user space.
    ///
    /// Returns `Err` if the `vaddr` and `len` do not represent a user space memory range.
    pub fn writer(&self, vaddr: Vaddr, len: usize) -> Result<VmWriter<'_, Fallible>> {
        Ok(self.root_vmar().vm_space().writer(vaddr, len)?)
    }

    /// Reads bytes into the destination `VmWriter` from the user space of the
    /// current process.
    ///
    /// If the reading is completely successful, returns `Ok`. Otherwise, it
    /// returns `Err`.
    ///
    /// If the destination `VmWriter` (`dest`) is empty, this function still
    /// checks if the current task and user space are available. If they are,
    /// it returns `Ok`.
    pub fn read_bytes(&self, src: Vaddr, dest: &mut VmWriter<'_, Infallible>) -> Result<()> {
        let copy_len = dest.avail();

        if copy_len > 0 {
            check_vaddr(src)?;
        }

        let mut user_reader = self.reader(src, copy_len)?;
        user_reader.read_fallible(dest).map_err(|err| err.0)?;
        Ok(())
    }

    /// Reads a value typed `Pod` from the user space of the current process.
    pub fn read_val<T: Pod>(&self, src: Vaddr) -> Result<T> {
        if core::mem::size_of::<T>() > 0 {
            check_vaddr(src)?;
        }

        let mut user_reader = self.reader(src, core::mem::size_of::<T>())?;
        Ok(user_reader.read_val()?)
    }

    /// Writes bytes from the source `VmReader` to the user space of the current
    /// process.
    ///
    /// If the writing is completely successful, returns `Ok`. Otherwise, it
    /// returns `Err`.
    ///
    /// If the source `VmReader` (`src`) is empty, this function still checks if
    /// the current task and user space are available. If they are, it returns
    /// `Ok`.
    pub fn write_bytes(&self, dest: Vaddr, src: &mut VmReader<'_, Infallible>) -> Result<()> {
        let copy_len = src.remain();

        if copy_len > 0 {
            check_vaddr(dest)?;
        }

        let mut user_writer = self.writer(dest, copy_len)?;
        user_writer.write_fallible(src).map_err(|err| err.0)?;
        Ok(())
    }

    /// Writes `val` to the user space of the current process.
    pub fn write_val<T: Pod>(&self, dest: Vaddr, val: &T) -> Result<()> {
        if core::mem::size_of::<T>() > 0 {
            check_vaddr(dest)?;
        }

        let mut user_writer = self.writer(dest, core::mem::size_of::<T>())?;
        Ok(user_writer.write_val(val)?)
    }

    /// Atomically loads a `PodAtomic` value with [`Ordering::Relaxed`] semantics.

// SPDX-License-Identifier: MPL-2.0

#![cfg_attr(not(test), no_std)]

/// An extension trait for Rust integer types, including `u8`, `u16`, `u32`,
/// `u64`, and `usize`, to provide methods to make integers aligned to a
/// power of two.
pub trait AlignExt {
    /// Returns to the smallest number that is greater than or equal to
    /// `self` and is a multiple of the given power of two.
    ///
    /// The method panics if `power_of_two` is not a
    /// power of two or is smaller than 2 or the calculation overflows
    /// because `self` is too large.
    ///
    /// # Examples
    ///
    /// ```
    /// use crate::align_ext::AlignExt;
    /// assert_eq!(12usize.align_up(2), 12);
    /// assert_eq!(12usize.align_up(4), 12);
    /// assert_eq!(12usize.align_up(8), 16);
    /// assert_eq!(12usize.align_up(16), 16);
    /// ```
    fn align_up(self, power_of_two: Self) -> Self;

    /// Returns to the greatest number that is smaller than or equal to
    /// `self` and is a multiple of the given power of two.
    ///
    /// The method panics if `power_of_two` is not a
    /// power of two or is smaller than 2 or the calculation overflows
    /// because `self` is too large. In release mode,
    ///
    /// # Examples
    ///
    /// ```
    /// use crate::align_ext::AlignExt;
    /// assert_eq!(12usize.align_down(2), 12);
    /// assert_eq!(12usize.align_down(4), 12);
    /// assert_eq!(12usize.align_down(8), 8);
    /// assert_eq!(12usize.align_down(16), 0);
    /// ```
    fn align_down(self, power_of_two: Self) -> Self;
}

macro_rules! impl_align_ext {
    ($( $uint_type:ty ),+,) => {
        $(
            impl AlignExt for $uint_type {
                #[inline]
                fn align_up(self, align: Self) -> Self {
                    assert!(align.is_power_of_two() && align >= 2);
                    self.checked_add(align - 1).unwrap() & !(align - 1)
                }

                #[inline]
                fn align_down(self, align: Self) -> Self {
                    assert!(align.is_power_of_two() && align >= 2);
                    self & !(align - 1)
                }
            }
        )*
    }
}

impl_align_ext! {
    u8,
    u16,
    u32,
    u64,
    usize,
}

# Summary

<!-- SUMMARY -->

## Maintainability

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 62

> ```diff
> 62 #[repr(i32)]
> 63 #[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, TryFromInt)]
> 64 #[expect(non_camel_case_types)]
> 65 /// This definition is the same from linux
> 66 pub enum MadviseBehavior {
> ```

`alphabetical-attrs` (nit): The outer attributes on `MadviseBehavior` are not sorted alphabetically: `#[repr(i32)]` appears before `#[derive(...)]` and `#[expect(...)]`. This makes the enum differ from the Rust-specific attribute ordering guideline.

**Fix.** Order the outer attributes alphabetically, with `#[derive(...)]` last as the guideline specifies. For example, place `#[expect(non_camel_case_types)]` before `#[repr(i32)]`, and keep the sorted `#[derive(Clone, Copy, Debug, TryFromInt)]` after them.

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 65

> ```diff
> 62 #[repr(i32)]
> 63 #[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, TryFromInt)]
> 64 #[expect(non_camel_case_types)]
> 65 /// This definition is the same from linux
> 66 pub enum MadviseBehavior {
> ```

`rfc1574-summary` (nit): The doc summary for `MadviseBehavior` is not a noun phrase for the enum, lacks terminal punctuation, and says `from linux` instead of clearly describing what API contract the enum represents.

**Fix.** Rewrite the first doc line as a concise noun phrase with punctuation, for example `/// Linux-compatible advice values for the `madvise` system call.`

## Correctness

### `kernel/src/syscall/capget.rs` line 48

> ```diff
> 38     // Annoying legacy format with 64-bit capabilities exposed as two sets of 32-bit fields,
> 39     // so we need to split the capability values up.
> 40     let result = cap_user_data_t {
> 41         // Note we silently drop the upper capabilities here.
> 42         // This behavior is considered fail-safe behavior.
> 43         effective: effective_capset.as_u32(),
> 44         permitted: permitted_capset.as_u32(),
> 45         inheritable: inheritable_capset.as_u32(),
> 46     };
> 47 
> 48     user_space.write_val(cap_user_data_addr, &result)?;
> ```

Incorrect syscall result (major): `capget()` accepts `LINUX_CAPABILITY_VERSION_3`, but writes only one `cap_user_data_t`. Version 3 exposes 64-bit capability sets as two 32-bit records, so capabilities above bit `31` are silently omitted and the second userspace record remains unchanged.

**Fix.** Write the two-element version-3 result: low 32 bits in `data[0]` and high 32 bits in `data[1]` for `effective`, `permitted`, and `inheritable`.

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 30

> ```diff
> 23     if len > isize::MAX as usize {
> 24         return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EINVAL, "len align overflow");
> 25     }
> ...
> 30     let len = len.align_up(PAGE_SIZE);
> ```

Reachable panic (major): `len.align_up(PAGE_SIZE)` can panic even after the `len > isize::MAX` check. For example, on a 64-bit build, a userspace `len` of `isize::MAX` passes the check, then `align_up()` calls `checked_add(PAGE_SIZE - 1).unwrap()` and panics because the addition overflows.

**Fix.** Use checked alignment before continuing, and return a syscall error on overflow. For example, compute `let len = len.checked_add(PAGE_SIZE - 1).ok_or_else(|| Error::with_message(Errno::EINVAL, "len align overflow"))? & !(PAGE_SIZE - 1);` or add a fallible alignment helper.

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 40

> ```diff
> 35     match behavior {
> 36         MadviseBehavior::MADV_NORMAL
> 37         | MadviseBehavior::MADV_SEQUENTIAL
> 38         | MadviseBehavior::MADV_WILLNEED => {
> 39             // perform a read at first
> 40             let mut buffer = vec![0u8; len];
> 41             ctx.user_space()
> 42                 .read_bytes(start, &mut VmWriter::from(buffer.as_mut_slice()))?;
> 43         }
> ```

Reachable allocation failure (major): `MADV_NORMAL`, `MADV_SEQUENTIAL`, and `MADV_WILLNEED` allocate a `Vec` with one byte per advised byte. A caller can pass a page-aligned range close to `isize::MAX`, so `vec![0u8; len]` can exhaust kernel memory or panic before `read_bytes()` returns an error.

**Fix.** Shared with the security finding at `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 30: do not allocate a buffer covering the whole user range. Treat advice values that need no action as no-ops, or prefault the range in bounded chunks/pages so allocation size is independent of user-controlled `len`.

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 48

> ```diff
> 44         MadviseBehavior::MADV_DONTNEED => {
> 45             warn!("MADV_DONTNEED isn't implemented, do nothing for now.");
> 46         }
> 47         MadviseBehavior::MADV_FREE => madv_free(start, end, ctx)?,
> 48         _ => todo!(),
> 49     }
> ```

Reachable panic (major): `MadviseBehavior::try_from()` accepts valid advice values such as `MADV_RANDOM`, `MADV_REMOVE`, and `MADV_PAGEOUT`, but the wildcard arm reaches `todo!()`. A userspace call like `madvise(addr, PAGE_SIZE, MADV_RANDOM)` will panic the kernel instead of returning a syscall error or applying the advice.

**Fix.** Shared with the security finding at `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 48: validate `behavior` to the implemented set at the syscall boundary. Replace `todo!()` with implemented behavior or an error return for unsupported advice values, for example `return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EINVAL, "unsupported madvise behavior")`.

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 57

> ```diff
> 53 fn madv_free(start: Vaddr, end: Vaddr, ctx: &Context) -> Result<()> {
> 54     let user_space = ctx.user_space();
> 55     let root_vmar = user_space.root_vmar();
> 56     let advised_range = start..end;
> 57     let _ = root_vmar.remove_mapping(advised_range);
> 58 
> 59     Ok(())
> ```

Incorrect memory advice semantics (major): `MADV_FREE` removes the mapping with `root_vmar.remove_mapping(advised_range)`. `MADV_FREE` is supposed to leave the virtual mapping valid and only allow the physical pages to be discarded under memory pressure; after this call, a later access to the same address can fault as unmapped instead of seeing the original or reclaimed page contents.

**Fix.** Preserve the VM mapping for `MADV_FREE`. Implement lazy-free page handling in the existing mapping/page-cache layer, or return an unsupported-advice error until that behavior exists.

## Security

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 30

> ```diff
> 23     if len > isize::MAX as usize {
> 24         return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EINVAL, "len align overflow");
> 25     }
> ...
> 30     let len = len.align_up(PAGE_SIZE);
> ...
> 39             // perform a read at first
> 40             let mut buffer = vec![0u8; len];
> 41             ctx.user_space()
> 42                 .read_bytes(start, &mut VmWriter::from(buffer.as_mut_slice()))?;
> ```

`validate-at-boundaries` (major): `len` is a user-controlled syscall argument, but after only an `isize::MAX` cap it is used as the exact size of a kernel allocation for `MADV_NORMAL`, `MADV_SEQUENTIAL`, and `MADV_WILLNEED`. A hostile process can call `madvise(page_aligned_addr, isize::MAX, MADV_NORMAL)` and drive `vec![0u8; len]` into a huge kernel allocation before `read_bytes()` does any useful validation of the user range, giving an unprivileged denial of service.

**Fix.** Shared with the correctness finding at `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 40: do not allocate a kernel buffer proportional to the user-supplied range. Validate the range at the syscall boundary, then prefault or probe it page-by-page with a bounded scratch buffer, or return `EINVAL`/`ENOMEM` for ranges above a small internal limit.

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 48

> ```diff
> 35     match behavior {
> 36         MadviseBehavior::MADV_NORMAL
> 37         | MadviseBehavior::MADV_SEQUENTIAL
> 38         | MadviseBehavior::MADV_WILLNEED => {
> ...
> 47         MadviseBehavior::MADV_FREE => madv_free(start, end, ctx)?,
> 48         _ => todo!(),
> 49     }
> ```

`validate-at-boundaries` (critical): `behavior` is user supplied, and `MadviseBehavior::try_from()` accepts values such as `MADV_RANDOM`, `MADV_REMOVE`, or `MADV_POPULATE_WRITE`, but the syscall then reaches `todo!()` for those valid enum variants. A hostile process can pass `behavior = 1` (`MADV_RANDOM`) with a page-aligned address and nonzero length and trigger a kernel panic instead of getting a controlled syscall error.

**Fix.** Shared with the correctness finding at `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 48: validate `behavior` to the implemented set at the syscall boundary. Return `EINVAL` or another appropriate syscall error for unsupported advice values instead of keeping them as enum variants that fall into `todo!()`.

## Documentation

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 47

> ```diff
> 44         MadviseBehavior::MADV_DONTNEED => {
> 45             warn!("MADV_DONTNEED isn't implemented, do nothing for now.");
> 46         }
> 47         MadviseBehavior::MADV_FREE => madv_free(start, end, ctx)?,
> 48         _ => todo!(),
> ```

`linux-compat-docs` (major): `sys_madvise()` now handles `MadviseBehavior::MADV_FREE`, but `book/src/kernel/linux-compatibility/limitations-on-system-calls/memory-management.md` still lists `MADV_FREE` under unsupported advice and the documented supported SCML only includes `MADV_NORMAL`, `MADV_SEQUENTIAL`, and `MADV_WILLNEED`. That leaves the Linux Compatibility docs stale for the newly supported `madvise` behavior.

**Fix.** Update the Linux Compatibility `madvise` coverage in `book/src/kernel/linux-compatibility/limitations-on-system-calls/memory-management.md` so `MADV_FREE` is documented according to the final behavior, and update the matching SCML coverage artifact if this tree keeps one for syscall coverage.

 # Summary
 
-<!-- SUMMARY -->
+The main risks are in `sys_madvise()`: several user-controlled inputs can panic or exhaust kernel memory, and the new `MADV_FREE` arm currently unmaps memory instead of preserving the mapping with lazy-free semantics. `sys_capget()` also accepts capability version 3 while returning only the low 32-bit capability word, so newer capability bits are lost.
 
+The highest-priority fixes are to replace the reachable `todo!()` with controlled advice validation, make `madvise` length alignment and probing bounded/fallible, and either implement real `MADV_FREE` semantics or report it as unsupported. After the runtime behavior is settled, update the Linux compatibility docs to match it. I did not find a concrete issue in `kernel/src/syscall/open.rs`.
+
 ## Maintainability
 
 ### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 62
@@ -39,7 +41,11 @@
 
 `rfc1574-summary` (nit): The doc summary for `MadviseBehavior` is not a noun phrase for the enum, lacks terminal punctuation, and says `from linux` instead of clearly describing what API contract the enum represents.
 
-**Fix.** Rewrite the first doc line as a concise noun phrase with punctuation, for example `/// Linux-compatible advice values for the `madvise` system call.`
+**Fix.** Rewrite the first doc line as a concise noun phrase with punctuation, for example:
+
+```rust
+/// Linux-compatible advice values for the `madvise` system call.
+```

 # Summary
 
-<!-- SUMMARY -->
+The main risks are in `sys_madvise()`: several user-controlled inputs can panic or exhaust kernel memory, and the new `MADV_FREE` arm currently unmaps memory instead of preserving the mapping with lazy-free semantics. `sys_capget()` also accepts capability version 3 while returning only the low 32-bit capability word, so newer capability bits are lost.
 
+The highest-priority fixes are to replace the reachable `todo!()` with controlled advice validation, make `madvise` length alignment and probing bounded/fallible, and either implement real `MADV_FREE` semantics or report it as unsupported. After the runtime behavior is settled, update the Linux compatibility docs to match it. I did not find a concrete issue in `kernel/src/syscall/open.rs`.
+
 ## Maintainability
 
 ### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 62
@@ -39,7 +41,11 @@
 
 `rfc1574-summary` (nit): The doc summary for `MadviseBehavior` is not a noun phrase for the enum, lacks terminal punctuation, and says `from linux` instead of clearly describing what API contract the enum represents.
 
-**Fix.** Rewrite the first doc line as a concise noun phrase with punctuation, for example `/// Linux-compatible advice values for the `madvise` system call.`
+**Fix.** Rewrite the first doc line as a concise noun phrase with punctuation, for example:
+
+```rust
+/// Linux-compatible advice values for the `madvise` system call.
+```

# Summary

The main risks are in `sys_madvise()`: several user-controlled inputs can panic or exhaust kernel memory, and the new `MADV_FREE` arm currently unmaps memory instead of preserving the mapping with lazy-free semantics. `sys_capget()` also accepts capability version 3 while returning only the low 32-bit capability word, so newer capability bits are lost.

The highest-priority fixes are to replace the reachable `todo!()` with controlled advice validation, make `madvise` length alignment and probing bounded/fallible, and either implement real `MADV_FREE` semantics or report it as unsupported. After the runtime behavior is settled, update the Linux compatibility docs to match it. I did not find a concrete issue in `kernel/src/syscall/open.rs`.

## Maintainability

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 62

> ```diff
> 62 #[repr(i32)]
> 63 #[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, TryFromInt)]
> 64 #[expect(non_camel_case_types)]
> 65 /// This definition is the same from linux
> 66 pub enum MadviseBehavior {
> ```

`alphabetical-attrs` (nit): The outer attributes on `MadviseBehavior` are not sorted alphabetically: `#[repr(i32)]` appears before `#[derive(...)]` and `#[expect(...)]`. This makes the enum differ from the Rust-specific attribute ordering guideline.

**Fix.** Order the outer attributes alphabetically, with `#[derive(...)]` last as the guideline specifies. For example, place `#[expect(non_camel_case_types)]` before `#[repr(i32)]`, and keep the sorted `#[derive(Clone, Copy, Debug, TryFromInt)]` after them.

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 65

> ```diff
> 62 #[repr(i32)]
> 63 #[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, TryFromInt)]
> 64 #[expect(non_camel_case_types)]
> 65 /// This definition is the same from linux
> 66 pub enum MadviseBehavior {
> ```

`rfc1574-summary` (nit): The doc summary for `MadviseBehavior` is not a noun phrase for the enum, lacks terminal punctuation, and says `from linux` instead of clearly describing what API contract the enum represents.

**Fix.** Rewrite the first doc line as a concise noun phrase with punctuation, for example:

```rust
/// Linux-compatible advice values for the `madvise` system call.
```

## Correctness

### `kernel/src/syscall/capget.rs` line 48

> ```diff
> 38     // Annoying legacy format with 64-bit capabilities exposed as two sets of 32-bit fields,
> 39     // so we need to split the capability values up.
> 40     let result = cap_user_data_t {
> 41         // Note we silently drop the upper capabilities here.
> 42         // This behavior is considered fail-safe behavior.
> 43         effective: effective_capset.as_u32(),
> 44         permitted: permitted_capset.as_u32(),
> 45         inheritable: inheritable_capset.as_u32(),
> 46     };
> 47 
> 48     user_space.write_val(cap_user_data_addr, &result)?;
> ```

Incorrect syscall result (major): `capget()` accepts `LINUX_CAPABILITY_VERSION_3`, but writes only one `cap_user_data_t`. Version 3 exposes 64-bit capability sets as two 32-bit records, so capabilities above bit `31` are silently omitted and the second userspace record remains unchanged.

**Fix.** Write the two-element version-3 result: low 32 bits in `data[0]` and high 32 bits in `data[1]` for `effective`, `permitted`, and `inheritable`.

> ```diff
> 23     if len > isize::MAX as usize {

 # Summary
 
-<!-- SUMMARY -->
+The main risks are in `sys_madvise()`: several user-controlled inputs can panic or exhaust kernel memory, and the new `MADV_FREE` arm currently unmaps memory instead of preserving the mapping with lazy-free semantics. `sys_capget()` also accepts capability version 3 while returning only the low 32-bit capability word, so newer capability bits are lost.
 
+The highest-priority fixes are to replace the reachable `todo!()` with controlled advice validation, make `madvise` length alignment and probing bounded/fallible, and either implement real `MADV_FREE` semantics or report it as unsupported. After the runtime behavior is settled, update the Linux compatibility docs to match it. I did not find a concrete issue in `kernel/src/syscall/open.rs`.

131:Incorrect memory advice semantics (major): `MADV_FREE` removes the mapping with `root_vmar.remove_mapping(advised_range)`. `MADV_FREE` is supposed to leave the virtual mapping valid and only allow the physical pages to be discarded under memory pressure; after this call, a later access to the same address can fault as unmapped instead of seeing the original or reclaimed page contents.
135:## Security
152:`validate-at-boundaries` (major): `len` is a user-controlled syscall argument, but after only an `isize::MAX` cap it is used as the exact size of a kernel allocation for `MADV_NORMAL`, `MADV_SEQUENTIAL`, and `MADV_WILLNEED`. # Expected defects

1. location: kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs lines 35-50 (persona: development, grounding: unsupported MADV behavior must not panic, severity: major)
   defect: `MadviseBehavior` defines `MADV_NOHUGEPAGE`, but the match in `sys_madvise` has no arm for it. The value therefore reaches the catch-all `_ => todo!()` branch. A Podman call using `madvise(..., MADV_NOHUGEPAGE)` causes a kernel panic even though this Asterinas build does not support huge pages and can safely treat the advice as a no-op.
   MATCH IF: A reviewer should flag the reachable `todo!()` panic for the declared `MADV_NOHUGEPAGE` value and require a non-panicking unsupported-operation behavior, such as warning and returning success.

2. location: kernel/src/syscall/capget.rs lines 20-49 (persona: development, grounding: match capget version negotiation and null-data semantics, severity: major)
   defect: `sys_capget` immediately returns `EINVAL` when the requested capability version is unsupported, without writing `LINUX_CAPABILITY_VERSION_3` back through the header pointer. It also unconditionally writes the capability result to `cap_user_data_addr`, so a null data pointer cannot return success. The Linux capget contract uses an unsupported-version call to report the supported version, and permits a null data pointer for the version-query case. The base implementation therefore breaks Podman's capability probing protocol.
   MATCH IF: A reviewer should flag both missing parts of the version-query contract: the supported version must be written back on an unsupported request, and a null data pointer must not be dereferenced or rejected when the call is only querying the ABI version.

3. location: kernel/src/syscall/open.rs lines 21-40 (persona: development, grounding: reject empty pathname for openat, severity: major)
   defect: `sys_openat` reads the pathname and passes it directly to `FsPath::new` and the resolver. With an empty pathname, it does not return the required `ENOENT` error at the syscall boundary and instead relies on lower-level path handling, which does not provide the Linux `openat` behavior expected by Podman. `openat` has no `AT_EMPTY_PATH` option that would make an empty pathname valid.
   MATCH IF: A reviewer should flag that an empty pathname reaches generic path resolution and require `sys_openat` to return `ENOENT` explicitly before resolution.

# Summary

The main risks are in `sys_madvise()`: several user-controlled inputs can panic or exhaust kernel memory, and the new `MADV_FREE` arm currently unmaps memory instead of preserving the mapping with lazy-free semantics. `sys_capget()` also accepts capability version 3 while returning only the low 32-bit capability word, so newer capability bits are lost.

The highest-priority fixes are to replace the reachable `todo!()` with controlled advice validation, make `madvise` length alignment and probing bounded/fallible, and either implement real `MADV_FREE` semantics or report it as unsupported. After the runtime behavior is settled, update the Linux compatibility docs to match it. I did not find a concrete issue in `kernel/src/syscall/open.rs`.

## Maintainability

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 62

> ```diff
> 62 #[repr(i32)]
> 63 #[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, TryFromInt)]
> 64 #[expect(non_camel_case_types)]
> 65 /// This definition is the same from linux
> 66 pub enum MadviseBehavior {
> ```

`alphabetical-attrs` (nit): The outer attributes on `MadviseBehavior` are not sorted alphabetically: `#[repr(i32)]` appears before `#[derive(...)]` and `#[expect(...)]`. This makes the enum differ from the Rust-specific attribute ordering guideline.

**Fix.** Order the outer attributes alphabetically, with `#[derive(...)]` last as the guideline specifies. For example, place `#[expect(non_camel_case_types)]` before `#[repr(i32)]`, and keep the sorted `#[derive(Clone, Copy, Debug, TryFromInt)]` after them.

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 65

> ```diff
> 62 #[repr(i32)]
> 63 #[derive(Debug, Clone, Copy, TryFromInt)]
> 64 #[expect(non_camel_case_types)]
> 65 /// This definition is the same from linux
> 66 pub enum MadviseBehavior {
> ```

`rfc1574-summary` (nit): The doc summary for `MadviseBehavior` is not a noun phrase for the enum, lacks terminal punctuation, and says `from linux` instead of clearly describing what API contract the enum represents.

**Fix.** Rewrite the first doc line as a concise noun phrase with punctuation, for example:

```rust
/// Linux-compatible advice values for the `madvise` system call.
```

## Correctness

### `kernel/src/syscall/capget.rs` line 48

> ```diff
> 38     // Annoying legacy format with 64-bit capabilities exposed as two sets of 32-bit fields,
> 39     // so we need to split the capability values up.
> 40     let result = cap_user_data_t {
> 41         // Note we silently drop the upper capabilities here.
> 42         // This behavior is considered fail-safe behavior.
> 43         effective: effective_capset.as_u32(),
> 44         permitted: permitted_capset.as_u32(),
> 45         inheritable: inheritable_capset.as_u32(),
> 46     };
> 47 
> 48     user_space.write_val(cap_user_data_addr, {prompt}result)?;
> ```

Incorrect syscall result (major): `capget()` accepts `LINUX_CAPABILITY_VERSION_3`, but writes only one `cap_user_data_t`. Version 3 exposes 64-bit capability sets as two 32-bit records, so capabilities above bit `31` are silently omitted and the second userspace record remains unchanged.

**Fix.** Write the two-element version-3 result: low 32 bits in `data[0]` and high 32 bits in `data[1]` for `effective`, `permitted`, and `inheritable`.

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 30

> ```diff
> 23     if len > isize::MAX as usize {
> 24         return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EINVAL, "len align overflow");
> 25     }
> ...
> 30     let len = len.align_up(PAGE_SIZE);
> ```

Reachable panic (major): `len.align_up(PAGE_SIZE)` can panic even after the `len > isize::MAX` check. For example, on a 64-bit build, a userspace `len` of `isize::MAX` passes the check, then `align_up()` calls `checked_add(PAGE_SIZE - 1).unwrap()` and panics because the addition overflows.

**Fix.** Use checked alignment before continuing, and return a syscall error on overflow. For example, compute `let len = len.checked_add(PAGE_SIZE - 1).ok_or_else(|| Error::with_message(Errno::EINVAL, "len align overflow"))? {prompt} !(PAGE_SIZE - 1);` or add a fallible alignment helper.

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 40

> ```diff
> 35     match behavior {
> 36         MadviseBehavior::MADV_NORMAL
> 37         | MadviseBehavior::MADV_SEQUENTIAL
> 38         | MadviseBehavior::MADV_WILLNEED => {
> 39             // perform a read at first
> 40             let mut buffer = vec![0u8; len];
> 41             ctx.user_space()
> 42                 .read_bytes(start, {prompt}mut VmWriter::from(buffer.as_mut_slice()))?;
> 43         }
> ```

Reachable allocation failure (major): `MADV_NORMAL`, `MADV_SEQUENTIAL`, and `MADV_WILLNEED` allocate a `Vec` with one byte per advised byte. A caller can pass a page-aligned range close to `isize::MAX`, so `vec![0u8; len]` can exhaust kernel memory or panic before `read_bytes()` returns an error.

**Fix.** Shared with the security finding at `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 30: do not allocate a buffer covering the whole user range. Treat advice values that need no action as no-ops, or prefault the range in bounded chunks/pages so allocation size is independent of user-controlled `len`.

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 48

> ```diff
> 44         MadviseBehavior::MADV_DONTNEED => {
> 45             warn!("MADV_DONTNEED isn't implemented, do nothing for now.");
> 46         }
> 47         MadviseBehavior::MADV_FREE => madv_free(start, end, ctx)?,
> 48         _ => todo!(),
> 49     }
> ```

Reachable panic (major): `MadviseBehavior::try_from()` accepts valid advice values such as `MADV_RANDOM`, `MADV_REMOVE`, and `MADV_PAGEOUT`, but the wildcard arm reaches `todo!()`. A userspace call like `madvise(addr, PAGE_SIZE, MADV_RANDOM)` will panic the kernel instead of returning a syscall error or applying the advice.

**Fix.** Shared with the security finding at `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 48: validate `behavior` to the implemented set at the syscall boundary. Replace `todo!()` with implemented behavior or an error return for unsupported advice values, for example `return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EINVAL, "unsupported madvise behavior")`.

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 57

> ```diff
> 53 fn madv_free(start: Vaddr, end: Vaddr, ctx: {prompt}Context) -> Result<()> {
> 54     let user_space = ctx.user_space();
> 55     let root_vmar = user_space.root_vmar();
> 56     let advised_range = start..end;
> 57     let _ = root_vmar.remove_mapping(advised_range);
> 58 
> 59     Ok(())
> ```

Incorrect memory advice semantics (major): `MADV_FREE` removes the mapping with `root_vmar.remove_mapping(advised_range)`. `MADV_FREE` is supposed to leave the virtual mapping valid and only allow the physical pages to be discarded under memory pressure; after this call, a later access to the same address can fault as unmapped instead of seeing the original or reclaimed page contents.

**Fix.** Preserve the VM mapping for `MADV_FREE`. Implement lazy-free page handling in the existing mapping/page-cache layer, or return an unsupported-advice error until that behavior exists.

## Security

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 30

> ```diff
> 23     if len > isize::MAX as usize {
> 24         return_errno_with_message!(Errno::EINVAL, "len align overflow");
> 25     }
> ...
> 30     let len = len.align_up(PAGE_SIZE);
> ...
> 39             // perform a read at first
> 40             let mut buffer = vec![0u8; len];
> 41             ctx.user_space()
> 42                 .read_bytes(start, {prompt}mut VmWriter::from(buffer.as_mut_slice()))?;
> ```

`validate-at-boundaries` (major): `len` is a user-controlled syscall argument, but after only an `isize::MAX` cap it is used as the exact size of a kernel allocation for `MADV_NORMAL`, `MADV_SEQUENTIAL`, and `MADV_WILLNEED`. A hostile process can call `madvise(page_aligned_addr, isize::MAX, MADV_NORMAL)` and drive `vec![0u8; len]` into a huge kernel allocation before `read_bytes()` does any useful validation of the user range, giving an unprivileged denial of service.

**Fix.** Shared with the correctness finding at `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 40: do not allocate a kernel buffer proportional to the user-supplied range. Validate the range at the syscall boundary, then prefault or probe it page-by-page with a bounded scratch buffer, or return `EINVAL`/`ENOMEM` for ranges above a small internal limit.

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 48

> ```diff
> 35     match behavior {
> 36         MadviseBehavior::MADV_NORMAL
> 37         | MadviseBehavior::MADV_SEQUENTIAL
> 38         | MadviseBehavior::MADV_WILLNEED => {
> ...
> 47         MadviseBehavior::MADV_FREE => madv_free(start, end, ctx)?,
> 48         _ => todo!(),
> 49     }
> ```

`validate-at-boundaries` (critical): `behavior` is user supplied, and `MadviseBehavior::try_from()` accepts values such as `MADV_RANDOM`, `MADV_REMOVE`, or `MADV_POPULATE_WRITE`, but the syscall then reaches `todo!()` for those valid enum variants. A hostile process can pass `behavior = 1` (`MADV_RANDOM`) with a page-aligned address and nonzero length and trigger a kernel panic instead of getting a controlled syscall error.

**Fix.** Shared with the correctness finding at `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 48: validate `behavior` to the implemented set at the syscall boundary. Return `EINVAL` or another appropriate syscall error for unsupported advice values instead of keeping them as enum variants that fall into `todo!()`.

## Documentation

### `kernel/src/syscall/madvise.rs` line 47

> ```diff
> 44         MadviseBehavior::MADV_DONTNEED => {
> 45             warn!("MADV_DONTNEED isn't implemented, do nothing for now.");
> 46         }
> 47         MadviseBehavior::MADV_FREE => madv_free(start, end, ctx)?,
> 48         _ => todo!(),
> ```

`linux-compat-docs` (major): `sys_madvise()` now handles `MadviseBehavior::MADV_FREE`, but `book/src/kernel/linux-compatibility/limitations-on-system-calls/memory-management.md` still lists `MADV_FREE` under unsupported advice and the documented supported SCML only includes `MADV_NORMAL`, `MADV_SEQUENTIAL`, and `MADV_WILLNEED`. That leaves the Linux Compatibility docs stale for the newly supported `madvise` behavior.

**Fix.** Update the Linux Compatibility `madvise` coverage in `book/src/kernel/linux-compatibility/limitations-on-system-calls/memory-management.md` so `MADV_FREE` is documented according to the final behavior, and update the matching SCML coverage artifact if this tree keeps one for syscall coverage.
